## Page 1


Thermodynamics formalism for singular flows
Ming Li and Xingzhong Liu
Abstract
We establish that C∞three-dimensional flows with positive topological entropy admit only finitely
many ergodic measures of maximal entropy, even when singularities (zero-velocity points) are present.
Furthermore, every ergodic measure of maximal entropy is rapid mixing for such flows within a C∞
open and dense subset. To prove this, we develop a novel symbolic coding system for flows with
singularities, which serves as a fundamental tool in this work. We also define the strong positive
recurrence (SPR) property for singular flows and verify that SPR flows can be coded by suspension
flows of SPR symbolic systems. This framework extends to other singular flows, including star flows,
and to equilibrium states.
2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: 37C40, 37C10, 37D25, 37D35, 37B10, 37C30, 37A25.
Keywords: singular flows, symbolic dynamics, equilibrium states, strong positive recurrence, rapid
mixing.

Introduction
The ergodic theory of dynamical systems fundamentally seeks to characterize typical long-term behav-
ior through invariant probability measures. For flows, this pursuit becomes particularly challenging when
singularities–equilibrium points where the velocity field vanishes–are present. In this paper, we develop
a novel symbolic framework to characterize the statistical properties of singular flows with non-uniform
hyperbolicity. Applied to three-dimensional smooth flows, this yields the following result:
Theorem 1.1. Every C∞flow on a closed 3-dimensional smooth Riemannian manifold M 3 with positive
topological entropy has only finitely many ergodic measures of maximal entropy.
Moreover, there exists a C∞open and dense subset of the set of all C∞flows with positive topological
entropy on M 3 such that, for every flow in this subset, every ergodic measure of maximal entropy is rapid
mixing.
Here the notion rapid mixing denotes superpolynomial decay of correlations: for any n ∈Z+, there
exist C > 0 and m ≥1 such that for all observables F, G ∈Cm(M),

holds for all t ∈R.
1.1
Singular flows
Throughout this paper, we assume that M is a smooth closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
d ≥3, that is, M is compact and without boundary. A C1 vector field X on M generates a C1 flow
φt : M →M.
Compared to the case of diffeomorphisms, studying flows presents greater challenges. A central diffi-
culty arises from the presence of singularities (zero-velocity points of the flow). A flow with singularities
is called a singular flow. In this paper, we also use singular flow to generically refer to all flows–both
with and without singularities–to emphasize that singularities are permitted.
While singularities themselves support trivial dynamics as fixed points of the flow, the dynamics often
become highly complex when recurrent orbits accumulate near them. This complexity can disrupt stan-
dard dynamical structures, as exemplified by the classical Lorenz attractor [85]. Due to its singularities,
the Lorenz attractor is neither uniformly hyperbolic nor structurally stable, yet exhibits robust dynamical
behavior.
It has long been recognized that hyperbolicity in flows manifests in transverse directions rather than
the flow direction. To analyze the hyperbolic structure of flows, Liao [74] defined the linear Poincar´e
flow on normal bundles. However, this flow remains undefined at singularities, which typically destroys
2


## Page 3


compactness.
Moreover, near singularities, the flow speed approaches zero, causing orbits to spend
significant time there. Simultaneously, both the domain of the Poincar´e map and the influence range of
the linear Poincar´e flow tend to vanish.
To address the compactness issue, [69] introduced the extended linear Poincar´e flow, extending its
definition to singularities. This framework was later extended to the nonlinear Poincar´e flow [46]. For
the second challenge, Liao [75, 77, 78] pioneered scaling techniques, subsequently refined by [52, 120] and
others. Nevertheless, these techniques do not resolve all difficulties associated with singular flows. In this
paper, building upon previous works, we develop a novel approach for studying the statistical properties
of singular flows.
If there exists a global section for the flow on the manifold, the corresponding return map is a
diffeomorphism. It is generally accepted that the dynamics of such a flow resemble those of its discrete
return map.
However, global sections typically do not exist, which requires studying dynamics near
periodic orbits through local sections. This is particularly challenging for flows with singularities, where no
return map induced by finitely many sections can have uniformly bounded return times. The foundation
of our work lies in newly constructing a collection of sections that is potentially infinite in total number
but locally finite, with return times uniformly bounded above and below.
1.2
Equilibrium states
The statistical properties of dynamical systems explain how the random behaviors of a system are
determined. This constitutes a fundamental problem in dynamical systems theory and is closely related
to the evolution of measures. One of the most important concepts in the statistical theory of dynamical
systems is the measure of maximal entropy, or more generally, the equilibrium state, defined as an invariant
probability measure achieving the supremum in the variational principle.
Specifically, we say that a probability measure µ on M is X-invariant (resp. X-ergodic) if µ is φt-
invariant (resp. φt-ergodic) for any t ∈R. Denote by P(X) (resp. Pe(X)) the set of all X-invariant (resp.
X-ergodic) probability measures.
For any µ ∈P(X), the metric entropy for X with respect to µ, denote by hµ(X), is defined as the
metric entropy for the time-1 map φ1 with respect to µ. According to the variational principle (see, e.g.
[118]),
htop(X) = sup{hµ(X) : µ ∈P(X)} = sup{hµ(X) : µ ∈Pe(X)},
where htop(X) is the topological entropy for X. The measure of maximum entropy (MME) for X is an
X-invariant probability measures µ such that hµ(X) = htop(X).
A potential is a bounded measurable function ϑ defined on M. The metric pressure of ϑ for X with
respect to µ ∈P(X) is defined as
Pµ(X, ϑ) = hµ(X) +
Z
ϑdµ.
Similarly, by the variational principle,
Ptop(X, ϑ) = sup{Pµ(X, ϑ) : µ ∈P(X)} = sup{Pµ(X, ϑ) : µ ∈Pe(X)},
where Ptop(X, ϑ) is the topological pressure for X. The equilibrium state of ϑ for X is a measure µ ∈P(X)
such that Pµ(X, ϑ) = Ptop(X, ϑ). The MME corresponds to the equilibrium state of ϑ ≡0.
The existence and finiteness of ergodic equilibrium states for dynamical systems constitute a long-
standing question. Research on equilibrium states began with uniformly hyperbolic systems [100, 2, 20,
114, 22, 23, 101] and then extended to non-uniformly hyperbolic systems. Substantial results have been
established for various types of non-uniformly hyperbolic systems.
There are two primary approaches to addressing this problem. The first employs the specification
property. Bowen [23] pioneered this method to show the existence and uniqueness of equilibrium state
for uniformly hyperbolic transitive sets. Subsequently, Climenhaga-Thompson [42] developed this theory,
establishing existence and uniqueness for certain non-uniformly hyperbolic systems (see, e.g. [41, 42, 43,
40]), including geodesic flows on closed manifolds with nonpositive curvature [28].
3


## Page 4


Most recently, Pacifico-Yang-Yang further generalized the techniques of Climenhaga-Thompson to
flows with singularities [96, 99], proving that for C1-open and dense star flows satisfying the pressure gap
condition (see §1.7), ergodic equilibrium states exist and are finite [97, 98].
In this article, we employ a different approach: constructing a finite-to-one semi-conjugacy from
a topological Markov flow to the flow on the manifold (or a topological Markov shift to a diffeomor-
phism), then leveraging properties of symbolic dynamics (see, e.g. [58]) to study the equilibrium states
of flows. This method has been widely applied (see [31, 38, 37, 80, 83, 82] for instance). Recently, Buzzi-
Crovisier-Sarig established the finiteness of equilibrium states for smooth surface diffeomorphisms [34].
The corresponding result was later extended to three-dimensional flows without singularities [123, 27].
1.3
Symbolic dynamics for non-uniformly hyperbolic systems
The symbolic dynamics approach to studying hyperbolic behavior of dynamical systems has achieved
significant success. This includes applications to uniformly hyperbolic systems [2, 113, 105, 19, 106, 24]
and piecewise maps on intervals and beyond [63, 31, 32].
In Sarig’s groundbreaking work [110], symbolic dynamics was constructed for non-uniformly hyperbolic
surface diffeomorphisms. The method has seen substantial development in recent years, both for various
non-uniformly hyperbolic discrete systems [80, 17, 79, 6] and for non-uniformly hyperbolic flows without
singularities [83, 33, 81].
In this paper, our central approach involves the construction of symbolic dynamics for general non-
uniformly hyperbolic flows, possibly singular.
A topological Markov shift (Σ, σ) is a symbolic system generated by a directed graph with at most
countably many vertices. A roof function r is a uniformly bounded positive continuous function defined
on Σ. A topological Markov flow (Σr, σr) is the suspension flow generated by a topological Markov shift
(Σ, σ) and a roof function r on it. Under the Bowen-Walters metric, this flow is continuous.
The regular set of a topological Markov flow is the set of points whose symbolic coordinate sequence has
at least one symbol that repeats infinitely often in the future and at least one (possibly different) symbol
that repeats infinitely often in the past. A topological Markov flow is local compact if its generating graph
has finite out-degree and in-degree at every vertex. An irreducible component of a topological Markov
flow is a maximal subset of Σr where any two states in the generating graph are connected by directed
paths. See definitions in §5.1
An X-invariant probability measure µ on M is χ-hyperbolic if, for µ-almost every point, the absolute
values of all Lyapunov exponents except that of the flow direction exceed χ. A measure µ is hyperbolic if
there exists χ > 0 such that µ is χ-hyperbolic. A measure µ is regular if µ(Sing(X)) = 0, where Sing(X)
is the set of all singularities of X. Two regular hyperbolic measures µ and ν are homoclinically related if
for µ-almost every x and ν-almost every y, the stable manifold of x transversely intersects the unstable
manifold of y, and conversely.
Theorem A. Let X be a C1+β vector field (β > 0) on M. For any χ > 0, there exists a locally compact
topological Markov flow (bΣbr, bσbr) and a map c
πbr : bΣbr →M satisfying the following properties:
(1) c
πbr ◦bσt
br = φt ◦c
πbr for all t ∈R.
(2) sup br < ∞and inf br > 0. Both br and c
πbr are H¨older continuous with respect to the Bowen-Walters
metric.
(3) c
πbr(bΣ#
br ) has full measure for every χ-hyperbolic regular X-invariant measure.
(4) c
πbr is finite-to-one on bΣ#

br which lifts all
χ-hyperbolic regular ergodic measures homoclinically related to µ.
4


## Page 5


See Theorem 11.1 and 11.2 for more details. To the best of our knowledge, Theorem A constitutes
the first symbolic coding result for general singular flows that is finite-to-one with uniformly bounded
return times. We note that in [9], a symbolic system was constructed for the geometric Lorenz attractors
(or general singular hyperbolic attractors) with singularities, where the corresponding return times are
unbounded.
A flow is called Bernoulli if its time-1 map is a Bernoulli automorphism. We say that a flow is Bernoulli
up to a period if it is Bernoulli, or isomorphic to the product of a Bernoulli flow and a rotational flow.
Denote by PerT (X) the number of periodic orbits with minimal positive period less than T.
As an application of Theorem A, one may study equilibrium states of singular flows via symbolic
systems.
The following theorem (see Theorem 12.1 for the stronger version) is a direct corollary of
Theorem A and [67, Theorem 1.1], [83, Theorem 4.7 and 5.1].
Theorem B. Let X be a C1+β vector field (β > 0) on M. Then for any H¨older continuous potential
ϑ on M and any hyperbolic regular ergodic measure µ, there is at most one hyperbolic regular ergodic
equilibrium state of ϑ for X homoclinically related to µ. If it exists, it must be Bernoulli up to a period.
In particular, there exists at most one hyperbolic regular ergodic MME for X homoclinically related
to µ. If there exists at least one MME, then there is C0 > 0 such that PerT (X) ≥C0eT htop(X)/T for any
T large enough.
1.4
Strong positive recurrence
For uniformly hyperbolic systems, coding by finite symbolic systems provides deep statistical insights
via the spectral gap of the Ruelle operator. The situation changes significantly for non-uniformly hy-
perbolic systems, where many fundamental questions remain open. In their groundbreaking work [36],
Buzzi-Crovisier-Sarig introduced the concept of Strong Positive Recurrence (SPR) for diffeomorphisms.
SPR diffeomorphisms are generic in settings such as smooth surface dynamics and exhibit rich statistical
properties.
The concept of SPR, or its equivalent conditions, originated in the study of countable Markov shifts
through works by Gurevich, Zargaryan, Savchenko [61, 59, 60], Vere-Jones [117], Sarig [109], and others.
SPR symbolic systems possess strong statistical properties.
Notably, Cyr-Sarig established that the
Ruelle operator exhibits a spectral gap for topologically mixing SPR countable Markov shifts [44].
In [36], Buzzi-Crovisier-Sarig developed the SPR theory for diffeomorphisms. By coding SPR diffeo-
morphisms with SPR topological Markov shift, they transferred the profound statistical properties arising
from spectral gaps in symbolic dynamics to non-uniformly hyperbolic diffeomorphisms. This work con-
structs a refined ergodic theory framework for a broad class of non-uniformly hyperbolic diffeomorphisms,
comparable to that of uniformly hyperbolic systems. It proves that such diffeomorphisms possess key
statistical properties including but not limited to exponential mixing, large deviation principles, and
central limit theorems.
In this paper, we define the SPR property for singular flows. It should be noted that due to potential
singularities, we employ the scaled linear Poincar´e flow to construct Pesin blocks (see Definition 5.4).
Furthermore, since singularities may lie in the closure of Pesin blocks–preventing their extension to
compact closures–we adapt the SPR definition accordingly for singular flows.
Specifically, we say that a flow is strongly positively recurrent (SPR) for a potential ϑ, if there exists
χ > 0 satisfying: for each ε > 0, there exists a (χ, ε)-Pesin block, a compact subset Λ of this Pesin block,
and numbers P0 < Ptop(X, ϑ), τ > 0 such that ν(Λ) > τ for every ergodic measure ν with Pν(X, ϑ) > P0.
We also verify that SPR singular flows can be coding by suspension flows of some SPR topological Markov
shift. As an immediate application, we obtain the following theorem. A detailed statement appears in
Theorem 12.3.
Theorem C. Let M be a closed smooth Riemannian manifold, X be a C1+β vector field (β > 0) on M
and ϑ be a H¨older continuous potential on M. If X is SPR for ϑ, then there exist only finitely many
ergodic equilibrium states of ϑ for X, and each is hyperbolic, regular and Bernoulli up to a period.

1.5
Rate of mixing
For SPR diffeomorphisms, a key consequence of the spectral gap is that ergodic measures of maxi-
mal entropy are exponential mixing [36] (see also [86] for ergodic equilibrium states of smooth surface
diffeomorphisms satisfying certain conditions). However, mixing properties differ fundamentally between
flows and diffeomorphisms, even for Anosov systems.
Although Anosov flows exhibit strong mixing
in transverse derictions, exponential mixing for such flows saw limited progress prior to Dolgopyat’s
groundbreaking work [49]. This work inspired subsequent research on Anosov flows and geodesic flows on
negative curvature manifolds, see [104, 84, 55, 11, 115, 30, 68] for instance. Recently, Tsujii-Zhang proved
exponential mixing of ergodic equilibrium states for three-dimensional smooth mixing Anosov flows [116].
The situation changes substantially for Axiom A flows. Although they remain exponentially mixing in
transverse directions, Ruelle constructed examples of mixing Axiom A flows that fail to be exponentially
mixing [107]. Subsequently, Pollicott proved that mixing rates for mixing basic sets can be arbitrarily slow
[103]. Dolgopyat developed a standard technique and proved that typical Axiom A flows are rapid mixing
[50]. Field-Melbourne-T¨or¨ok [51] introduced methods to construct good asymptotics under shadowing
and perturbations, establishing rapid mixing for ergodic equilibrium states of open and dense Axiom A
flows. Dolgopyat’s approach was also extended to prove rapid mixing of SRB measures for nonuniformly
hyperbolic flows [89] in the sense of Young towers [121, 122]. Building on this, [90, 13] employed new
techniques to broaden the applicability of prior results from [89], while [8] proved rapid mixing for SRB
measures of singular hyperbolic flows.
For suspension semi-flows over interval maps with infinitely many intervals, Baladi-Vall´e introduced
a method to prove exponential mixing for SRB measures under uniform non-integrability conditions [12].
This method was extended in [10] and used to study Teichm¨uller flows. Subsequently, Ara´ujo-Melbourne
further developed the approach for Lorenz attractors, establishing exponential mixing for SRB measures
when the stable bundle is sufficient smooth [7].
And [48] proved the exponential mixing for Gibbs
measures of Axiom A attractors.
In this work, we study rapid mixing for ergodic equilibrium states of general H¨older potentials for
SPR topological Markov flows.
Extending Field-Melbourne-T¨or¨ok’s methods of good asymptotics to
singular flows, we prove that ergodic equilibrium states having good asymptotics for SPR singular flows
are necessarily rapid mixing. (See Theorem 14.16 for details.)
1.6
Three-dimensional flows
The thermodynamic formalism for two-dimensional diffeomorphisms has seen substantial development
recently, see [110, 34, 35, 26, 36, 39, 27, 86] for instance. Corresponding results for three-dimensional
nonsingular flows appear in [83, 33, 27, 123].
As an application of the framework built in this paper, Theorem D below shows that three-dimensional
smooth flows are SPR for H¨older continuous potentials ϑ with small variation Var(ϑ) := supx∈M 3 ϑ(x) −
infx∈M 3 ϑ(x). This implies the existence and finiteness of ergodic equilibrium states. See also Theo-
rem 15.1 and Corollary 15.6 for a more precise version.
The small variation condition Var(ϑ) < htop(X) ensures two crucial properties: it ensures that invari-
ant measures with large pressure (including equilibrium states) are hyperbolic, and satisfies the pressure
gap condition at singularities (see details in §1.7).
Theorem D. Let M 3 be a closed 3-dimensional smooth Riemannian manifold and X be a C∞vector
field on M 3. If ϑ is a H¨older continuous potential on M 3 satisfying Var(ϑ) < htop(X), then X is SPR
for ϑ. As a corollary, there exist only finitely many ergodic equilibrium states of ϑ for X.
In particular, if X has positive topological entropy, then there exist only finitely many ergodic MMEs.
Furthermore, we prove that for C∞-open and dense three-dimensional vector fields satisfying the
small variation condition, each ergodic equilibrium state has good asymptotics, thereby exhibiting rapid
mixing. Specifically, let ϑ be a H¨older continuous potential on M3, denote by X ∞
ϑ (M 3) the set of all C∞
vector fields on M 3 satisfying Var(ϑ) < htop(X).

open and dense subset U of X ∞
ϑ (M 3) such that for every X ∈U, every ergodic equilibrium state of ϑ for
X is rapid mixing.
Theorem 1.1 is a special case of Theorems D and E when ϑ ≡0.
1.7
Star flows
The concept of the star system originates from the study of the renowned Stability Conjecture. During
research into this conjecture, Pliss, Liao, and Ma˜n´e observed a crucial condition: the system should not
generate non-hyperbolic periodic orbits under C1-small perturbations (and for flows, non-hyperbolic
singularities simultaneously). Liao named this the star condition. The star condition appears quite weak;
however, for diffeomorphisms, it implies Axiom A combined with the no-cycle condition. This result was
first established for the two-dimensional case by Liao [76] and Ma˜n´e [87], and later extended to general
dimensions by Ma˜n´e [88], Aoki [5] and Hayashi [62]. Finally, Gan and Wen proved an analogous result
for flows without singularities [53].
For flows with singularities, Axiom A fails when singularities are approximated by periodic orbits.
The geometric Lorenz attractor [56, 3, 57] serves as a canonical example: although not structurally
stable, it exhibits robustness under perturbations. To characterize such hyperbolicity, Morales-Pujals-
Pacifico introduced the notion of singular hyperbolicity [92], later extended to higher dimensions through
sectional hyperbolicity in [91].
Counterexamples constructed in [47] show that singular hyperbolicity
does not hold for generic star flows. To address this, Bonatti-da Luz introduce the concept of multi-
singular hyperbolicity in [18], and prove via methods from [111] that all C1-open and dense star flows are
multi-singular hyperbolic.
Recently, Pacifico-Yang-Yang established that C1-open and dense star flows satisfying the pressure
gap condition supx∈Sing(X) ϑ(x) < Ptop(X), where ϑ is a given potential, admit only finitely many ergodic
equilibrium states [98]. Since Sing(X) is compact, the pressure gap condition is equivalent to that none
of the atomic measures on the singularities are equilibrium states. By the upper-semicontinuity of metric
entropies, the pressure gap condition also ensure that large pressure measures do not accumulate to
singularity.
On the other hand, this pressure gap condition is sharp: Shi-Yang constructed examples showing that
for certain singular star flows and potentials, atomic measures at singularities become equilibrium states,
which may lead to multiple ergodic equilibrium states within a same chain recurrent class [112]. In fact,
SPR of countable symbolic system means the existence of pressure gap between the whole system and at
infinity, while in the scaling sense, singularities can be regarded as the manifold’s boundary at infinity –
both sharing identical essence.
As another application for the coding of singular flows, we also establish rapid mixing for open and
dense star flows. Let ϑ be a H¨older continuous potential, denote by X ∗
ϑ(M) the set of all C2 star vector
fields satisfying supx∈Sing(X) ϑ(x) < Ptop(X).
Theorem F. (Theorem 16.4) For any H¨older continuous potential ϑ, there exists a C2 open and C1
dense subset U of X ∗
ϑ(M) such that for every X ∈U, every ergodic equilibrium state of ϑ is rapid mixing.
1.8
Plan of the proof
For non-singular flows, it is common to discretize the flow by constructing a return system generated
from finitely many sections. However, for flows with singularities, using only finitely many sections leads
to return times that can become arbitrarily large. Here, we introduce a novel return map f generated by
a countable collection of sections {Dj}j∈Z+, with uniformly bounded return times. Crucially, this system
of sections is locally finite: for each section Dj, the number of sections intersecting with f(Dj) or f −1(Dj)
is uniformly bounded. Consequently, the local compactness arising from this locally finite structure can
substitute for the role played by the global compactness of finite section systems in the coding process.
7


## Page 8


This local compactness is precisely the core reason enabling successful coding for non-uniformly hyperbolic
systems.
Another issue in coding occurs near singularities, where both the size of the sections and the scope of
influence of the hyperbolicity of the return map tend to vanish. This challenge is commonly faced when
handling singular flows. Liao introduced an effective approach: scaling the corresponding linear map to
achieve certain uniform estimates. Here, we also employ the scaled return map to the aforementioned
newly constructed sections for coding.
The scaling process can be viewed as normalizing the metric on the tangent space at non-singular
points through multiplication by the reciprocal of the local flow speed. This operation expands the size
(in the sense of metric) of hyperbolic influence near singularities to match its range in regions far from
singularitie. Correspondingly, Pesin theory for non-uniformly hyperbolic systems achieves uniform hyper-
bolicity by switching to the Lyapunov metric. We thus carry out two sequential metric modifications: first
ensuring uniform local sizing via scaling, then establishing uniform hyperbolicity through the Lyapunov
metric.
Subsequently, we extend the framework in [33] by applying our newly constructed sections and the
scaled system defined above to code singular flows. Concurrently, we implement irreducible coding for
Borel homoclinic classes of singular flows using the method from [34], and perform SPR coding for SPR
singular flows following the approach in [36]. As an application, we prove that three-dimensional smooth
flows and star flows satisfying the small variation condition or the pressure gap condition are SPR. The
existence and finiteness of ergodic equilibrium states follow directly from the SPR property.
The SPR symbolic system possesses a spectral gap for the Ruelle operator, which serves as the foun-
dation for our proof of rapid mixing. We adopt Dolgopyat’s approach from [50], where he established
several equivalent conditions for rapid mixing in suspension flows of finite type symbolic systems. How-
ever, these results are not directly applicable in our setting for two key reasons: the norm used here to
obtain the spectral gap differs from the standard H¨older norm, and more critically, the infinite-alphabet
environment leads to the absence of crucial uniform properties such as the global Gibbs property. To
address this, we first introduce a new norm to derive a Lasota-Yorke type inequality. For the lack of
uniformity, we restrict the phase cancellation condition to a predetermined subsystem constituted by
finite alphabets, thereby enabling the application of the local Gibbs property.
When applying the above results to three-dimensional smooth flows and star flows, we employ the
method introduced by Field-Melbourne-T¨or¨ok in [51]. This approach was originally developed to prove
rapid mixing for open and dense Axiom A flows. The core technique involves constructing sequences of
periodic orbits satisfying the phase cancellation condition through shadowing and perturbations. Cru-
cially, these periodic orbits lie within a small neighborhood of a transverse homoclinic orbit to some
hyperbolic periodic orbit, and are contained in a uniformly hyperbolic set. This allows us to lift these
periodic orbits to a finite type symbolic subsystem, thereby confining the phase cancellation within a
determinable finite alphabets range, ultimately yielding the rapid mixing.
1.9
Outline
This paper is organized into four parts.
In Part I, we construct a Poincar´e return system induced by at most countably many locally finite
sections that covers all non-singular points. The local finiteness provides the foundation for coding of
singular flows.
In §2, we recall fundamental concepts, including Poincar´e maps and scale Poincar´e maps, and results
of uniform estimates.
In §3, we complete the construction of the aforementioned Poincar´e return system.
In §4, we analyze the return maps and their scaling on the constructed sections, and build a global
linear flow compatible with the return dynamics.
In Part II, we coding singular flows following Buzzi-Crovisier-Lima’s methodology, leveraging the
return system constructed in Part I.
In §5, we review some related concepts and results.
8


## Page 9


In §6–10, we give the estimates required for coding.
In §11, we prove the coding theorems for singular flows, including irreducible coding and SPR coding.
In Part III, we employ the constructed coding system to transfer statistical properties from topological
Markov flows to singular flows.
In §12, we address the existence and finiteness of ergodic equilibrium states.
In §13, we discuss the transfer operator of one-sided symbolic dynamics and prove a Lasota-Yorke
type inequality.
In §14, we prove rapid mixing of ergodic equilibrium states having good asymptotics for SPR flows.
In Part IV, we apply aforementioned results to three-dimensional smooth flows and star flows.
In §15, we show that three-dimensional smooth flows satisfying the small variation condition are
SPR of the potential, and every ergodic equilibrium state for C∞-open and dense vector fields has good
asymptotics.
In §16, we prove Theorem F for star flows satisfying the pressure gap condition.

Part I
Locally finite uniform Poincar´e sections
In this part, we construct a family of Poincar´e sections compatible with flows that may exhibit
singularities. Such sections and the corresponding first return maps form the foundational framework
for coding flows in Part II. A key property they possess is local finiteness. Additionally, we introduce a
global “linear Poincar´e flow” adapted to the Poincar´e section, which is required for the coding process.
2
Poincar´e maps and scaled Poincar´e maps
In this section, we recall the definitions of the Poincar´e maps and the scaled Poincar´e maps, along
with some properties.
2.1
Poincar´e maps
Recall that M is a smooth d-dimensional (d ≥3) connected compact Riemannian manifold without
boundary. Let X be a C1 vector field on M. It generates a C1 flow φt = φX
t
on M, together with the
tangent flow dφt : TM →TM on the tangent bundle TM.
Denote by Sing(X) the set of singularities of X. For any point x ∈M \ Sing(X), the normal space
Nx at x is defined as
Nx = N X

3
Compatible Poincar´e sections for singular flows
In this section, we only require that X is a C1 vector field. We aim to construct an appropriate set
of transverse sections. These sections and the corresponding Poincar´e return map will play a crucial role
in the coding process of Part II.
When X is devoid of singularities, the existence of standard Poincar´e sections is established in [21]
(also see [83]). We therefore focus here on cases where X contains singularities, although this construction
also applies to non-singular flows.
3.1
Poincar´e sections
We begin with introducing some definitions. A connected smooth codimension-one submanifold D ⊂
M \ Sing(X) is called a transverse section of X if each orbit of X intersects D transversely or does not
intersect with D.
We say that the disjoint union of at most countable many disjoint transverse sections D = U
j∈Γ Dj
(Γ is a finite set {1, 2, . . . , ℓ} or Z+) is a Poincar´e section of X if for any x ∈M \ Sing(X), we have
• when arranged in ascending order, the set {t > 0 : φt(x) ∈D} forms a one-sided sequence that
tends to positive infinity; and
11


## Page 12


• when arranged in descending order, the set {t < 0 : φt(x) ∈D} forms a one-sided sequence that
tends to negative infinity.
If D = U
j∈Γ Dj is a Poincar´e section of X, then one may define the roof function RD : D →R+ as
RD(x) = min{t > 0 : φt(x) ∈D}, and the Poincar´e return map fD : D →D as fD(x) = φRD(x)(x). In the
view of the Poincar´e section D, the flow φt on M \ Sing(X) can be regard as a suspension flow of the
Poincar´e return map fD with the roof function RD.
A Poincar´e section D of X is called uniform if infx∈D RD(x) > 0 and supx∈D RD(x) < +∞.
Definition 3.1. We say that a Poincar´e section D = U
j∈Γ Dj of X is locally finite if there exists a
constant Imax ≥1 such that for any j ∈Γ, we have
#{i ∈Γ : fD(Dj) ∩Di ̸= ∅or fD(Di) ∩Dj ̸= ∅} ≤Imax.
The locally finite property is of fundamental importance indeed in the coding process.
The following main result of this part asserts the existence of locally finite uniform Poincar´e sections
of X.
Theorem 3.2. For any C1 vector field X on M and any positive numbers δ > 0, κ > 1, there exist two
numbers r0 ∈(0, δ/κ), Imax ≥1 and at most countable many points {oj}j∈Γ ⊂M \Sing(X) such that for
every r ∈[r0, κr0],
(S1) Poincar´e section: {Noj(r|X(oj)|)}j∈Γ is a collection of mutually disjoint transverse sections, and
D(r) = U
j∈Γ Noj(r|X(oj)|) is a Poincar´e section of X;
(S2) Uniformity: RD(r)(x) ≥κr0 and RD(r)(x) ≤δ for all x ∈D(r), where RD(r) is the corresponding
roof function of D(r);
(S3) Local finiteness: #{i ∈Γ : fD(r)(Noj(r|X(oj)|)) ∩Noi(r|X(oi)|) ̸= ∅or f −1
D(r)(Noj(r|X(oj)|)) ∩
Noi(r|X(oi)|) ̸= ∅} ≤Imax for any j ∈Γ, where fD(r) is the corresponding Poincar´e return map of
D(r);
(S4) Separated: M(oj, 3κr0|X(oj)|) ∩M(oi, 3κr0|X(oi)|) = ∅for any j ̸= i;

Clearly, if sections are permitted to intersect, we may choose Npi(r′|X(pi)|) from the above lemma,
which can form a Poincar´e system. However, the return time may lack a uniform positive lower bound.
Next, we construct sections satisfying the theorem’s requirements based on the above Npi(r′|X(pi)|).
For non-singular flows (where Γ0 is a finite set), Lima-Sarig in [83] provided a construction method.
Due to the local finiteness in the above lemma, this method is still applicable here after adjustment.
Although the proof need frequent switching between sections and may seem cumbersome, the core idea
is clear.
13


## Page 14


On each Npi(r′|X(pi)|), we define an ε-lattice net. Then, we push each lattice point along its orbit
for a short time (with is uniformly bounded), and consider a thin scaled tubular neighborhood of size
proportional to ε and longer length at each pushed lattice point. By Lemma 2.1, these new thin scaled
tubular neighborhoods still cover the non-singular set of M. Thus, we only need to push the lattice points
to suitable positions to ensure complete separation of the new small sections.
Note that if two lattice points could approach each other during pushing, their distance when flowing
to the same large section is less than a constant multiple of ε. Therefore, for each fixed lattice point, the
number of lattice points on the same Npi(r′|X(pi)|) at risk of approach is uniformly bounded. Combined
with the local finiteness of the above lemma, the total number at risk is uniformly bounded, even if the
total number of lattice points is infinite. Hence, a suitable push exists for sufficiently small ε.

4
Induced Poincar´e systems
As outlined in the introduction, we build on the coding method from [33, 81]. The key distinction lies
in handling infinite sections instead of finite ones, with the additional requirement that nearly all involved
objects must be scaled. Based on the locally finite Poincar´e sections constructed in §3, we establish a
foundational framework for return maps. This framework serves as the basis for the coding procedure in
Part II.

Part II
Symbolic dynamics for singular flows
5
Preliminaries
In this section, we recall some definitions and related results.
5.1
Topological Markov shifts and Topological Markov flows
5.1.1
Topological Markov shifts
Let G be a directed graph with vertex set V and edge set E = {u →v : u, v ∈V }. If every vertex
has positive ingoing degree and outgoing degree then say G is proper. Throughout the paper, G is always
assumed to be proper and have at most countable vertices.
If every vertex has finite degree then the graph G is called locally finite. A path of length n from u to
v is an n-tuple (u1, . . . , un) ∈V n such that u1 = u and un = v and ui →ui+1 for all 1 ≤i ≤n −1.
The graph G is called connected if for every u, v ∈V , there is a path from u to v. In this case, the
period of G is the integer gcd{n : u1 →· · · →un with u1 = un = a and a ∈V } which is independent of
the choice of a ∈V .
A maximal connected component G ′ of G is a connected sub-graph of G such that any other connected
subgraph of G does not contains G ′.
Define the symbolic space as
Σ = Σ(G ) = {x = {xn}n∈Z ∈V Z : xi →xi+1 for all i ∈Z},
endowed with the metric d(x, y) = exp[−min{|n| ∈Z : xn ̸= yn}]. Denote by σ : Σ →Σ the standard
left shift on Σ. The pair (Σ, σ) is called a topological Markov shift (TMS). We say that a TMS is locally
compact if V is locally finite. If V is finite then Σ is compact.
A path (u0, . . . , un) is also called an admissible word in Σ. And a cylinder is formed as
m[u0, . . . , un] := {x ∈Σ : xi+m = ui, 0 ≤i ≤n},
where (u0, . . . , un) is an admissible word. The regular set of Σ is
Σ# = {x ∈Σ : ∃u, v ∈V , xn = u for infinitely many n ∈Z+ and xn = v for infinitely many n ∈Z−}.
20


## Page 21


By the Poincar´e Recurrence theorem, µ(Σ#) = 1 for any σ-invariant probability measure µ.
A TMS (Σ, σ) is called irreducible if the graph G is connected. A subshift Σ′ ⊂Σ generated by a
proper, maximal connected component G ′ of G is called an irreducible component of Σ. In this case
(Σ′, σ|Σ′) is an irreducible TMS.
The period of an irreducible TMS is the period of G . An irreducible TMS with period 1 is called
irreducible and aperiodic. As a dynamical system, we have the following relations:
• σ : Σ →Σ is topologically transitive if and only if Σ is irreducible;
• σ : Σ →Σ is topologically mixing if and only if Σ is irreducible and aperiodic.
The following spectral decomposition of an irreducible TMS is well known (see, e.g. [36]).
Lemma 5.1. An irreducible TMS (Σ, σ) with period p > 1 can be decomposed into Σ = Up−1
i=0 Σi such
that each Σi is closed and σ(Σi) = Σi+1, where Σp = Σ0. Moreover, for each 0 ≤i ≤p −1, σp : Σi →Σi
is topologically conjugate to a topologically mixing TMS.
□
Denote by P(Σ) the set of σ-invariant probability measures, and by Pe(Σ) the set of ergodic σ-invariant
probability measures. Both of them are endowed with the standard weak-∗topology.
The Gurevich entropy of (Σ, σ) is defined as
hTOP(σ) := sup{hµ(σ) : µ ∈P(Σ)}.
A function ϑ : Σ →R is called
• locally β0-H¨older continuous if there is C > 0 such that |ϑ(u) −ϑ(v)| ≤C · d(u, v)β0 for all u, v ∈Σ
with u0 = v0.
• β0-H¨older continuous if it is bounded and locally β0-H¨older continuous.
For a H¨older continuous potential ϑ, the top pressure corresponding to ϑ is defined as
PTOP(σ, ϑ) := sup{Pµ(σ, ϑ) : µ ∈P(Σ)},
where Pµ(σ, ϑ) = hµ(σ) +
R
Σ ϑdµ is the metric pressure of the σ-invariant probability measure µ.
If the supreme is achieved by some probability measure µ, namely, Pµ(σ, ϑ) = PTOP(σ, ϑ), then µ is
called an equilibrium state for ϑ. In particular, if ϑ ≡0 then the top pressure is indeed the Gurevich
entropy hTOP(σ) of this TMS. In this case, the corresponding equilibrium state is called a measure of
maximal entropy. See [108] for more properties on the thermodynamic formalism of TMS.
5.1.2
Topological Markov flows
Let (Σ, σ) be a TMS. A roof function r is a bounded positive continuous function defined on Σ. For
n ≥1, the n-th Birkhoff sum of r is defined as rn = r + r ◦σ + · · · + r ◦σn−1. We also define r0 = 0 and
r−n := −rn ◦σ−n for any n > 0.
Define
Σr = {(x, t) : x ∈Σ and t ∈[0, r(x))}
and the flow σr on Σr acts as
σr,t(x, s) = (σn(x), s + t −rn(x))
where n is the unique integer satisfying rn(x) ≤s + t < rn+1(x).
The Bowen-Walters metric dr(·, ·) endows Σr such that σr constitutes a continuous flow [25]. The
pair (Σr, σr) is called a topological Markov flow (TMF). The regular set of Σr is
Σ#
r = {(v, t) ∈Σr : v ∈Σ#}.

that Σ′

TMS (Σ, σ) is topologically transitive. However, even though σ is topologically mixing, it is possible
that σr is not. By Lemma 5.1, every TMF with topologically transitive base can be recoded as one with
topologically mixing base. We just need to replace Σ by Σ0 and r by rp.
Denote by P(Σr) and Pe(Σr) the set of all σr-invariant probability measures and ergodic probability
measures, respectively. Both of them are endowed with the standard weak-∗topology.

Proposition 5.2. [14, 64] Let (Σr, σr) be a TMF and ϑ be a H¨older continuous potential on Σr with

5.2
Lyapunov exponents and hyperbolic measures
Let X be a C1 vector field on M. Recall that P(X) denotes the set of X-invariant Borel probability
measures, Pe(X) the set of ergodic X-invariant Borel probability measures, and a measure µ ∈P(X) is
called regular if µ(Sing(X)) = 0.
According to the Oseledec Theorem [95], there exists an invariant set O(X) ⊂M \ Sing(X) that has
full µ-measure for every regular X-invariant measure µ; for any x ∈O(X), there is an invariant splitting
for the tangent flow dφ:
TxM = E1

The numbers χ1(x), . . . , χk(x)(x) are called the Lyapunov exponents of x, and dj(x) is called the
multiplicity of λj(x). All functions k, χj and dj are measurable in x and invariant alone the orbit of x.
Moreover, if µ is ergodic, then k, χj and dj are constants µ-almost everywhere.

A regular X-invariant measure µ is called hyperbolic if, for µ-a.e. x, all Lyapunov exponents of x for
the linear Poincar´e flow ψ are non-zero. This is equivalent to requiring that the zero Lyapunov exponent
for the tangent flow dφ has multiplicity exactly one, which corresponds to the flow direction. For any
χ > 0, a regular X-invariant measure is called χ-hyperbolic if, for µ-a.e. x, all Lyapunov exponents of x
for ψ lie outside the interval [−χ, χ].

5.3
Pesin blocks and invariant manifolds
By (5.4), the tangent flow dφ typically lacks hyperbolicity along the flow direction. It is therefore
more natural to define Pesin blocks for flows using the linear Poincar´e flow. To achieve uniform estimates
in singular flows, we employ the scaled linear Poincar´e flow.

5.4
Homoclinic classes and Borel homoclinic classes
Assume that X is a C1+β vector field (β > 0) on M. Let x, y ∈PES. The transverse intersection of
W u(x) and W s(y) is the set
W u(x) ⋔W s(y) := {z ∈W u(x) ∩W s(y) : TzM = TzW u(x) + TzW s(y)}.
We say that x and y are homoclinically related, denote by x ∼y, if W u(x) ⋔W s(y) ̸= ∅and
W u(y) ⋔W s(x) ̸= ∅. The homoclinic relation is an equivalent relation on PES′ (see [36, Proposition 2.6]).
Each equivalence class of homoclinic relation ∼is called a Borel homoclinic class. This notion is first
introduced by Buzzi-Crovisier-Sarig for diffeomorphisms in [36].
The following properties of Borel homoclinic classes is proved in [36] for diffeomorphisms. The proof
of the flow case is similar.
Proposition 5.5. [36, Proposition 2.8] The following statements hold:
(1) The set of Borel homoclinic classes is a finite or countable partition of PES′ into X-invariant Borel
sets.
(2) Each Borel homoclinic class contains a dense set of hyperbolic periodic points.
(3) Any regular hyperbolic ergodic measure is carried by a Borel homoclinic class.
(4) Any measure carried by a Borel homoclinic class is regular and hyperbolic.
1In fact, the extended linear Poincar´e flow introduced in [69] allows us to lift Pesin blocks to compact extended Pesin
blocks on one-dimensional Grassmannian manifold. See [70, Lemma 4.4] for the details.
24


## Page 25


Let O be a periodic orbit. Denote by BHC(O) the Borel homoclinic class containing O. The classical
homoclinic class HC(O) of O defined by Newhouse [93] (which is called topological homoclinic classes in
[34]) is the set
HC(O) := {x ∈O′ : O′ is a hyperbolic periodic orbit s.t. O′ ∼O}.
Generally, the homoclinic class and the Borel homoclinic class of O do not coincide. We have HC(O) =
BHC(O).
The homoclinic relation can also be extended to regular hyperbolic measures. Precisely, let µ, ν be
two regular hyperbolic X-invariant measures. We say that µ is homoclinically related to ν (denoted by
µ ∼ν) if x ∼y for µ-a.e. x and ν-a.e. y. It follows from Proposition 5.5 that any two measures carried by
a common Borel homoclinic class are homoclinically related, and any two homoclinically related measures
are carried by the same Borel homoclinic class.
5.5
Equilibrium states of invariant sets
Let B be a measurable invariant set for the flow. Denote by P(X|B) (resp. Pe(X|B)) be the set of
X-invariant (resp. X-ergodic) probability measures µ such that µ(B) = 1.
The top entropy for X on B is defined as
hTOP(X|B) = sup{hµ(X) : µ ∈P(X|B)}.
According to the variational principle (see, e.g. [118]), when B is compact, this top entropy equals the
topological entropy htop(X|B) for X on B. A measure of maximum entropy (MME) for X on B is an
X-invariant probability measure µ supported on B such that hµ(X) = hTOP(X|B).
Let ϑ be a potential on M. The top pressure of ϑ for X on B is defined as
PTOP(X|B, ϑ) = sup{Pµ(X, ϑ) : µ ∈P(X|B)}.
Similarly, according to the variational principle, if B is compact and ϑ is continuous, then the top pressure
is equal to the topological pressure Ptop(X|B, ϑ) for X on B. An equilibrium state of ϑ for X on B is a
measure µ ∈P(X|B) such that Pµ(X, ϑ) = PTOP(X|B, ϑ).
5.6
Strong positive recurrence
Let (Σ, σ) be a TMS. The strongly positive recurrent (SPR) property admits multiple equivalent
definitions. Here we present the extended version proposed in [36].
We say that (Σ, σ) is SPR for a H¨older continuous potential ϑ, if it has finite Gurevich entropy and
there exist a finite union of cylinders C and numbers P0 < PTOP(σ, ϑ), τ > 0 such that ν(C) > τ holds
for every ergodic X-invariant probability measure ν with Pν(σ, ϑ) > P0. In particular, we say (Σ, σ) is
SPR if it is SPR for ϑ ≡0.
Let (Σr, σr) be a TMF generated by a TMS (Σ, σ) and r be a roof function.
Analogous to the
definition of SPR property for TMS, we say that (Σr, σr) is SPR for a H¨older continuous potential ϑ if
the Gurevich entropy is finite, and there exists a finite union of cylinders C on Σ together with numbers
P0 < PTOP(σr, ϑ), τ > 0 such that
ν({(x, t) ∈(Σr, σr) : x ∈C, t ∈[0, r(x))}) > τ
holds for every ergodic X-invariant probability measure ν with Pν(σr, ϑ) > P0. We also say (Σr, σr) is
SPR if it is SPR for ϑ ≡0.
The SPR property of TMF (Σr, σr) is closely linked with that of the corresponding TMS (Σ, σ).
Proposition 5.6. A TMF (Σr, σr) is SPR for a H¨older continuous potential ϑ if and only if (Σ, σ) is
SPR for the H¨older continuous potential ∆ϑ −PTOP(σr, ϑ) · r.

We now turn to singular flows. Let X be a C1 vector field on M and B ⊂M be a invariant Borel
set for X which is not necessarily compact. We say that X (or the generated flow φ) is SPR on B
for a H¨older continuous potential ϑ : M →R if there exists χ > 0 satisfying the following property:
for each ε > 0, there are a (χ, ε)-Pesin block PESχ(ℓ, ε), a compact set Λ ⊂PESχ(ℓ, ε) and numbers
P0 < PTOP(X|B, ϑ), τ > 0 such that ν(Λ) > τ holds for every ν ∈Pe(X|B) with Pν(X, ϑ) > P0. In
particular, we say that X is SPR on B if it is SPR on B for ϑ ≡0. If B = M, we say that X is SPR
forϑ : M →R for simplicity.

Remark 6.11. Although the notations Q(x), q(x), p(x) and the aforementioned NUHχ, s(x), u(x), C(x)
do not have the superscript “∗”, please note that they characterize the hyperbolicity after scaling.
6.4
Scaled Pesin charts
In this subsection, we define the scaled Pesin charts for each x ∈D ∩NUHχ. Note that our definition
here differs slightly from common usage.
Typically, a small neighborhood of x in Dj is lifted to Hx via the exponential map expx at x, where
scaling is performed (since C(x) examines hyperbolicity after scaling) before lifting to Euclidean space
via C(x). Our approach instead lifts the entire section Dj (containing neighborhoods of x) to Hoj using
the exponential map expoj at the central point oj. Scaling occurs in this unified linear space Hoj, followed
by transferring to Hx through dx expoj, and final lift to Euclidean space via C(x).
This methodology provides some advantages: By treating exp−1
oj
as a natural coordinate map, we
transfer the analysis on submanifold Dj to the linear space Hoj, enabling direct handling of the scaled
systems. Consequently, existing results for diffeomorphisms and non-singular flows become applicable
while computational complexity reduces. We therefore define the following scaled Pesin charts.

7.2
Graph transforms
In this subsection we introduce graph transforms in order to construct invariant manifolds. Let us
reiterate that the scaling applied to Noj, along with the scaled hyperbolicity of the relevant mappings,
renders the discussion of graph transforms under scaled Pesin charts identical to that for diffeomorphisms
and non-singular flows. Therefore, we state the results of this subsection without proof, citing specific
references where appropriate. Furthermore, we state conclusions only for the stable direction, as the
unstable direction follows by complete symmetry.

8
First coding
In this section we construct a first coding. This coding is usually infinite to one.
8.1
Coarse graining
For any x ∈D∩NUH#
χ , there are uncountably many scaled double charts containing it simultaneously.
We need to select countably many of these for later use. The methods of selecting charts is first proposed
in [110].
In [33], the difficulty arising from the non-discrete nature of the flow’s time parameter is
addressed by relaxing the requirements of condition (GPO2) of Defination 7.1.
However, the situation we face here differs significantly: there are now infinitely many sections. Fortu-
nately, local finiteness ensures that the argument can proceed after appropriate modifications. Please see
the local discreteness in Proposition 8.1 (1) below. The conclusion obtained here is weaker than global
discreteness, but it suffices for subsequent applications.
Moreover, we wish to reiterate that in the following discussion we will exclusively consider the scaled
objects on the normal spaces Noj.

such that:

9
Inverse theorem
In this section, we investigate whether the quantification of hyperbolicity remains consistent at each
section when an orbit is shadowed by a gpo. Our approach largely follows in [33, § 6] and [81, § 6].
Up to now, most discussions primarily occurred in the normal space (except in Section 7.4). However,
this section necessitates a return to the manifold framework. This shift is required because the quan-
tification of hyperbolicity is evaluated along entire orbits rather than discretely at sections, which is a
groundbreaking feature introduced in [33]. Therefore, we will frequently handle transformations between
scaling multipliers at distinct points.
Define a roof function r : Σ →R by r(ev∗) := τx0(x) ≥inf RbD > 0 where ev∗= {eΨ∗,ps

xn(φrn(ev∗)(z)) = φrn+1(ev∗)(z). To
simplify notations, we denote zn := φrn(v∗)(z) for all n ∈Z.

10
Construction of the finite-to-one Topological Markov flow
In previous sections, we constructed a symbolic system (Σ, σ) with countably many states. Each state
corresponds to a gpo as defined in §7.1 and determines a unique point in bD = D(3r0) via the shadowing
lemma. Moreover, §9 shows that the quantification of hyperbolicity of the gpo and its shadowed point
are essentially identical.
This section aims to derive our main theorem from these results. To achieve this, we use methodologies
from [33] and [81].
Crucially, all parts required in their proof for non-singular flows have now been
established in our framework. We further note that most proofs are unaffected by the infiniteness of the
global section’s components. Consequently, we state the results without proofs.
Special attention must be given to Proposition 10.1 (2), which relies on the locally finiteness of bD.
This is a difference from non-singular flows.

11.2
Irreducible coding
In this subsection, we prove that mutually homoclinically related hyperbolic measures can be lifted
to the same irreducible component.
Theorem 11.2. Let M, X, χ > 0 and (bΣbr, bσbr) be as in Theorem 11.1. For any regular χ-hyperbolic
measure µ which is not supported on a single periodic orbit, there is an irreducible component bΣ′
br ⊂
bΣbr satisfying conditions (1,2) and (4-9) in Theorem 11.1.
The third statement of Theorem 11.1 is
strengthened to the following:
• Any regular χ-hyperbolic ergodic measure ν on M which is homoclinically related to µ has some lift
in bΣ′
br.
• The projection of any ergodic measure ν on bΣ′
br is homoclinically related to µ.
Proof. Proofs for diffeomorphisms and non-singular flows on three-dimensional compact manifolds are
provided in [34, Section 3] and [33, Section 10]. Here, we briefly outline the approaches and refer to their
works for details.
Employing the technique from Proposition 10.1 (3), we have the following lemma, which aligns with
[34, Proposition 3.6] and [33, Lemma 10.2].
Lemma 11.3. For any two ergodic measures supported on a common irreducible component of bΣbr, their
projections are regular hyperbolic ergodic measures that are homoclinically related.

Lemma 11.4. For any χ > 0, the set of ergodic measures on bΣbr whose projection is χ-hyperbolic is open

Lemma 11.5. There exists an irreducible component bΣ′

Proof. The set of all χ-hyperbolic periodic orbits homoclinically related to µ is countable. Let {Oi}i∈Z+
denote the collection of such orbits. By classical uniform hyperbolic theory, for each n ∈N, there exists
a compact transitive χ-hyperbolic set Kn containing O1, . . . , On. Theorem 11.1 (9) implies the existence
of a transitive compact set An ⊂bΣ#
br such that bπbr(An) = Kn and An contains some lifts of O1, . . . , On.
Since Theorem 11.1 (4) guarantees that O1 has finitely many lifts, there exist a subsequence {nk}k∈N
and a unique irreducible component of bΣbr containing every Ank. Thus it contains lifts of each {Oi}i∈N.
For technical details, see [34, Lemmas 3.11 and 3.12] and [33, Lemma 10.4].

11.3
SPR coding
The aim of this section is to lift an SPR flow on M to an SPR TMF.
Suppose that B = M or B ⊂M is a Borel homoclinic class, and in each case, B is SPR for a given
H¨older potential ϑ : M →R. The SPR property says that there exists ¯χ > 0 such that for any small
ε > 0, there is a Pesin block PES¯χ(ℓ, ε), a compact set Λ ⊂PES¯χ(ℓ, ε) and numbers P0 < P(φ|K),
τ ∈(0, 1) satisfying: if ν is an ergodic probability measure with Pν(φ, h) > P0 then ν(Λ) > τ.
By the definition of Pesin blocks (Definition 5.4), there exists ¯ℓ> ℓsuch that for any x ∈PES¯χ(ℓ, ε)
and |t| ≤sup br, we have φt(x) ∈PES¯χ(¯ℓ, ε). Take χ < ¯χ, let (bΣbr, bσbr) be the locally compact TMF given
by Theorem 11.1 or Corollary 11.7. Note that is B is a Borel homoclinic class, then (bΣbr, bσbr) is irreducible.
Next we show that this TMF satisfies the SPR property for ϑ ◦bπbr. The strategy follows from [36,
Proposition 10.4], in which they introduce a crucial property of symbolic models: χ-bornology. Since here
we just want to find an SPR coding, we only need the first part of their proof.
Theorem 11.8. Let B, (bΣbr, bσbr) and ϑ : M →R be as above. Then (bΣbr, bσbr) is SPR for the H¨older
potential ϑ ◦bπbr.
Proof. Proceeded as [36, Lemma 9.3], there exist numbers A, B > 0, depending on χ, ε and X, such that

Part III
Statistical properties of singular flows
The latter two parts present applications of the singular flow coding constructed in the first two
parts. Due to limited notations, we will reuse certain previously introduced notations but assign them
new meanings. The original meanings of these notations will not appear in the subsequent sections.
12
Finiteness of equilibrium states
In this section, we consider the finiteness of equilibrium states for singular flows and prove Theorem B
and C. We say that a vector field is C1+ if it is C1+β for some β > 0.
12.1
Equilibrium states on Borel homoclinic classs
Let B be a measurable invariant set for the flow and ϑ be a potential on M. Recall that an equilibrium
state of ϑ for X on B is a measure µ ∈P(X|B) such that Pµ(X, ϑ) = PTOP(X|B, ϑ). Denote by PerT (X|B)
the number of periodic orbits contained in B with minimal positive period less than T.
As showed in § 5.4, any two homoclinically related measures are carried by the same Borel homoclinic
class. Therefore, the following theorem is a stronger version of Theorem B.
Theorem 12.1. Let X be a C1+ vector field on M. Then for any H¨older continuous potential ϑ on M
and any Borel homoclinic class B of X, there is at most one regular hyperbolic ergodic equilibrium state
of ϑ for X on B. If it exists, it must be Bernoulli up to a period.
In particular, there exists at most one regular hyperbolic ergodic MME for X on B. If it exists, then
there is C > 0 such that PerT (X|B) ≥CeT ·hTOP(X|B)/T for any T large enough.
Proof. We first prove by contradiction that there is at most one regular hyperbolic ergodic equilibrium
state of ϑ for X on a Borel homoclinic class B. Suppose that µ and ν are two different regular ergodic
equilibrium state of ϑ for X on B, where µ is χ1-hyperbolic and ν is χ2-hyperbolic.
Denote χ =
min{χ1, χ2}.
By Theorem 11.2, there exists an irreducible component bΣ′
br of some TMF such that µ and ν have lifts
bµ and bν in it, respectively. Since hµ(X) = hbµ(bσbr) (Theorem 11.1 (3)) and
R
ϑd(bµ ◦bπ−1
br ) =
R
ϑ ◦bπbrdbµ,
both bµ and bν are equilibrium states of ϑ ◦bπbr for bσbr on the irreducible component bΣbr, where bπbr is given
by Theorem 11.1. This contradicts the uniqueness of equilibrium states for an irreducible component of
a TMF with a H¨older continuous roof function. See [83, Theorem 6.2] and [34, Corollary 3.3] for details.
Assume that µ is a regular hyperbolic ergodic equilibrium state of ϑ on B. Then bµ is an equilibrium
states of ϑ ◦bπbr for bσbr on bΣbr. It is proved in [67, Theorem 4.7 and 5.1] that bµ is Bernoulli up to a period,
and consequently µ shares this property.
60


## Page 61


Recall that the MMEs are the equilibrium states of ϑ ≡0. Thus there is at most one regular hyperbolic
ergodic MME for X on B. If there exists an MME on B, the growth rate estimate for periodic orbits
follows similarly from [83, Theorem 8.1].
□
Remark 12.2.
(1) The preceding proof shows that only H¨older continuity of ϑ◦bπbr is actually required.
It is introduced in [36] the notion of quasi-H¨older continuity, extending the applicability of corre-
sponding theorems to a wider class of potentials that include geometric potentials. Although the
normal bundle fails to be uniformly continuous for singular flows, it is H¨older continuous in the
scaled sense (see [70, § 4.2]). Since the projection map bπbr is also H¨older continuous, one may
similarly define quasi-H¨older continuity for singular flows.
(2) The coding provided by Theorem 11.2 cannot generally be used to derive upper bounds for the growth
rate of periodic orbits, since B may contain numerous periodic orbits with hyperbolicity weaker than
χ, which used in the coding process.
12.2
Equilibrium states for SPR sets
Now we consider the SPR case. The following theorem is a detailed version of Theorem C.
Theorem 12.3. Let X be a C1+ vector field on M and ϑ be a H¨older continuous potential on M.
• If X is SPR for ϑ on a Borel homoclinic class B, then there exists a unique equilibrium state of ϑ
for X on B, which is Bernoulli up to a period.
• If X is SPR for ϑ on M, then there exist only finitely many ergodic equilibrium states of ϑ for X,
and each is Bernoulli up to a period.
Proof. The first item follows from Theorem 11.8, Theorem 12.1 and [36, Theorem 11.6]. We focus on the
second one.
To prove by contradiction, assume that there exists a sequence of different ergodic equilibrium states
µn of ϑ for X. Since X is SPR for ϑ, there exists a Pesin block PESχ(ℓ, ε), a compact subset Λ ⊂
PESχ(ℓ, ε) and a constant τ ∈(0, 1) such that µn(Λ) > τ for all n.
This establishes that each µn
is regular. Consequently, by Proposition 5.5, every µn is carried by a Borel homoclinic class Bn, and
Theorem 12.1 shows that the Bn are mutually disjoint.
For each n, take xn ∈Bn ∩Λ. By passing to a subsequence if necessary, we assume xn →x ∈Λ. The
local stable/unstable manifolds have uniform size throughout Λ, and the angles between stable/unstable
subspaces are also uniform. Consequently, for sufficiently large indices i and j, the points xi and xj are
homoclinically related. This contradicts Bi ∩Bj = ∅, completing the proof.
□
13
Transfer operator of one-side TMS
The transfer operator (Ruelle operator) serves as a powerful tool for analyzing the statistical properties
of symbolic systems. In this section, we will examine the properties of the transfer operator for SPR
TMS.
13.1
Transfer operator, one-side TMS and spectral gap
13.1.1
One-sided TMS
Let G be a directed graph with vertex set V and edge set E = {u →v : u, v ∈V }. Assume that G is
proper (see Section 5.1) and has at most countable vertices. Define (recall that N0 = {0} ∪Z+)
Σ+ = Σ+(G ) = {x = (x0, x1, . . . ) ∈V N0 : xi →xi+1 for all i ∈N0},
61


## Page 62


which is endowed with a metric d(x, y) = e−min{n≥0:xi̸=yi} and an action of the left shift map σ. The
system (Σ+, σ) is called a one-sided TMS induced by the graph G . Since G is proper, Σ+ is locally
compact. Furthermore, if G is connected then Σ+ is called irreducible.
As before, denote by (Σ, σ) the two-sided TMS generated by G . Between (Σ, σ) and (Σ+, σ), there is
a natural semi-conjugacy π+ : Σ →Σ+ such that π+({un}n∈Z) = {un}n≥0.
This semi-conjugacy establishes a bijection between P(Σ) and P(Σ+) given by µ+ = µ◦(π+)−1. Both
µ+ and µ are invariant. They have the same entropy, and µ+ is ergodic (resp. mixing) if and only if µ is
ergodic (resp. mixing).
Moreover, every function h+ : Σ+ →R can be lifted to a one-side function h+ ◦π+ : Σ →R. We say
that a one-side TMS (Σ+, σ) is SPR for h+ : Σ+ →R, if the associated two-side TMS (Σ, σ) is SPR for
h+ ◦π+ : Σ →R.
Conversely, we have the following Sinai’s lemma (see [24]).
Lemma 13.1. For any β > 0, there is Cβ > 0 such that for every β-H¨older continuous potential h on
the two-sided TMS Σ, there are β/2-H¨older continuous functions h+ : Σ+ →R and ϱ : Σ →R such that
h+ ◦π+ = h + ϱ −ϱ ◦σ and satisfies ∥h+∥β/2 ≤Cβ ∥h∥β.
Let h on Σ and h+ on Σ+ be as in Lemma 13.1. It is easy to see that µ is an equilibrium state for h if
and only if µ+ is an equilibrium state for h+. And if the two-sided TMS (Σ, σ) is SPR for some potential
h, then the one-sided TMS (Σ+, σ) is SPR for the potential h+.
13.1.2
One-sided TMF
Let r : Σ+ →(0, ∞) be a roof function (see Section 5.1.2). Then the suspension semiflow (Σ+
r , σr) of
Σ+ with the roof function r is called a one-sided TMF.
Suppose (Σr, σr) is a two-sided TMF with a θ-H¨older continuous roof function r. Due to Lemma 13.1,
there are θ/2-H¨older continuous functions r+ : Σ+ →R and ϱ : Σ →R such that r+ ◦π+ = r + ϱ −ϱ ◦σ.
Note that it is not necessary a roof function on Σ+ since inf r+ may be non-positive.
To deal with this problem, one may replace σ by a sufficiently large σn. Then (r+◦π+)n = rn+ϱ−ϱ◦σn,
where (r+◦π+)n and rn are the n-th Birkhoff sum of r+◦π+ and r, respectively. Since inf rn ≥n inf r > 0
and ϱ is upper bounded, so (r+ ◦π+)n is away from zero for a large enough n. Note that (r+ ◦π+)n is
β/2-H¨older continuous and bounded away from infinity. Thus we get a roof function r+
n on the one-sided
TMS Σ+.
In what follows, we always fix such a large n ∈Z+ and consider the suspension flow over (Σ, σn) with
the roof function rn. Thus there is no loss of generality in assuming that both r and r+ are roof functions
on Σ and Σ+, respectively. Denote (Σ+
r , σr) the corresponding one-side TMF.

14
Rapid mixing for SPR Borel homoclinic classes with good
asymptotics
In this section, we show that the equilibrium state in a SPR Borel homoclinic class having good
asymptotics is rapid mixing (see Theorem 14.16).
14.1
Good asymptotics
In [51], Field-Melbourne-T¨or¨ok introduced the concept of good asymptotics to establish that open
and dense Axiom A flows are rapidly mixing. Let p ∈M be a hyperbolic periodic point and q be a
transverse homoclinic point of p. By the shadowing lemma, we may choose a sequence of periodic points
pN →q. Denote by τ0 and τN the periods of p and pN, respectively. It is proved in [51] that for any
vector field in a C1 open and Cα dense (α ≥1) set, there exist constants κ > 0, γ ∈(0, 1), α0 ∈[0, 2π),
S0 > 0 and sequences EN and ζN such that
τN = Nτ0 + κ + EN · γN cos(Nα0 + ζN) + o(γN)
(14.1)
where |EN| < S0 for every N ≥1 and either α0 = 0 and ζN ≡0, or α0 ∈(0, π) and ζN ∈(α0 −π/12, α0 +
π/12). We say that the periodic point p has good asymptotics if lim inf
N→∞|EN| > 0. Moreover, we say that
a Borel homoclinic class B has good asymptotics if it contains a periodic point having good asymptotics.
Proposition 14.1. [51] Let X be a Cα vector field (α ≥2) on M and p be a hyperbolic periodic point
of X with a transverse homoclinic point. Then there exist a C1 neighborhood U1 of X satisfying the
following property: there is a C2 open and Cα dense subset Uα in the set of all Cα vector fields in U1
such that for any Y ∈Uα, the continuation pY of p has good asymptotics.
Sketch of the Proof. Let U1 be a C1 neighborhood of X such that the continuation pY of p is well
defined for any Y ∈U1. Fix a transverse section Σ0 containing p and consider the Poincar´e return map

Lemma 14.3. There exists ρ0 ∈Z+ such that for all n ≥ρ0, all v1, v2 ∈V0, σn[v1] ∩[v2] ̸= ∅.

It follows from the above lemma that for any v1, v2 ∈V0, there is a loop of length ρ0 connecting
v1, v2 in the direct graph. We fix such a loop for each pair (v1, v2) and denote the collection of symbols
appeared in these loops by V1. Note that V1 is also finite and V0 ⊂V1.
It is proved in [51] that the presence of good asymptotics excludes the existence of approximate
eigenfunctions. And [50] establishes that the absence of approximate eigenfunctions implies rapid mixing.
However, applying this approach to our current coding of singular flows encounters several key challenges
arising from nonuniform hyperbolicity: the Gibbs property and norm ∥· ∥L both lack uniformity. To
address this, we must confine both the good asymptotics and approximate eigenfunctions within a local
region, thereby obtaining uniform control within these localized settings.

Theorem 14.8. [83] Assume that (Σr, σr) is an irreducible TMF and ϑ is a H ¨older continuous potential.
Then either every equilibrium measure of ϑ is mixing, or there is Σ′
r ⊂Σr of full measure such that

r, σr) is topologically conjugate to a TMF with constant roof function.
By employing a proof analogous to Proposition 14.4 but with simpler calculations, we may obtain the
following lemma.
Lemma 14.9. If (Σr, σr) is an irreducible TMF having good asymptotics, then it is topological mixing.

14.4
Rapid mixing for singular flows
Now we state the main result of this section.
Theorem 14.16. Let X be a C1+ vector field on M and ϑ be a H¨older continuous potential on M. If
X is SPR for ϑ on a Borel homoclinic class B and B has good asymptotics. Then the unique equilibrium
state of ϑ for X on B is rapid mixing.

Part IV
Applications
15
Three-dimensional flows
In this section, we consider C∞-smooth flows on a 3-dimensional closed manifold M 3.
15.1
SPR property for three-dimensional flows
Our objective in this subsection is to prove the following theorem.
Theorem D is an immediate
corollary of this theorem by taking K = M.
Theorem 15.1. Let X be a C∞-smooth vector field on M 3 and ϑ : M 3 →R be a H¨older continuous
function. If K is an X-invariant Borel set satisfying
• Var(ϑ) < htop(X|K) (which implies that htop(X|K) > 0); and
• PTOP(X|K, ϑ) = Ptop(X|K, ϑ),
then X is SPR for ϑ on K.
78


## Page 79


Fix a H¨older continuous function ϑ : M 3 →R and an X-invariant Borel set K satisfying the assump-
tion of Theorem 15.1, that is, Var(ϑ) < htop(X|K) and PTOP(X|K, ϑ) = Ptop(X|K, ϑ). We first state
some basic facts. Denote χ0 = 1
2(htop(X|K) −Var(ϑ)) > 0.
Lemma 15.2. We have the following properties.
• For any C1 vector field Y and any ν ∈Pe(Y ), if Pν(Y, ϑ) ≥Ptop(X|K, ϑ) −χ0, then ν is regular
and χ0-hyperbolic.
• If there are sequences of C∞vector fields Yn and νn ∈P(Yn) such that Yn →X in the C∞topology,
νn →µ ∈P(X|K) in the weak-∗topology and limn→∞Pνn(Yn, ϑ) ≥PTOP(X|K, ϑ), then µ is a
regular χ0-hyperbolic equilibrium state of ϑ for X on K.
Proof. By the variational principle, we have
Ptop(X|K, ϑ) =
sup
ν∈P(X|K)
(hν(X) +
Z
ϑdν) ≥
sup
ν∈P(X|K)
hν(X) + inf
x∈M 3 ϑ(x) = htop(X|K) + inf
x∈M 3 ϑ(x).
If ν ∈Pe(Y ) satisfies Pν(Y, ϑ) ≥Ptop(X|K, ϑ) −χ0, then we have
hν(Y ) ≥Pν(Y, ϑ) −sup
x∈M 3 ϑ(x) ≥Ptop(X|K, ϑ) −χ0 −sup
x∈M 3 ϑ(x)
≥htop(X|K) + inf
x∈M 3 ϑ(x) −sup
x∈M 3 ϑ(x) −χ0 = χ0 > 0.
It implies that the ergodic measure ν does not support on singularities. Thus ν is regular. Moreover,
the χ0-hyperbolicity of ν follows from the Ruelle’s inequality.
Next we assume that νn ∈P(Yn), Yn →X, νn →µ ∈P(X|K) and limn→∞Pνn(Yn, ϑ) ≥PTOP(X|K, ϑ).
By the upper-semicontinuity theorem of Newhouse [94], we know that lim supn→∞hνn(Yn) ≤hµ(X) and
then lim supn→∞Pνn(Yn, ϑ) ≤Pµ(X, ϑ). Thus Pµ(X, ϑ) ≥PTOP(X|K, ϑ).
On the other hand, it follows from µ ∈P(X|K) that Pµ(X, ϑ) ≤Ptop(X|K, ϑ). Since Ptop(X|K, ϑ) =
PTOP(X|K, ϑ), we obtain Pµ(X, ϑ) = PTOP(X|K, ϑ), which means that µ is an equilibrium state on K.
Then by the first item, µ is χ0-hyperbolic.
Finally we prove that µ is regular. If it is not true, then a := µ(Sing(X)) > 0. It follows that µ has a
decomposition µ = aµ0 + (1 −a)µ1, where µ0 ∈P(X|Sing(X)) and µ1 ∈P(X|K). Since
Pµ0(X) ≤hµ0(X) + sup
x∈M 3 ϑ(x) = sup
x∈M 3 ϑ(x), and
PTOP(X|K, ϑ) = Ptop(X|K, ϑ) ≥htop(X|K) + inf
x∈M 3 ϑ(x) = sup
x∈M 3 ϑ(x) + 2χ0,
we have
Pµ(X) = aPµ0(X) + (1 −a)Pµ1(X) ≤a(PTOP(X|K, ϑ) −2χ0) + (1 −a)PTOP(X|K, ϑ) < PTOP(X|K, ϑ).
This contradiction proves that µ is regular.
□
For every x ∈M 3, define
G1
x := {E ⊂TxM 3 : E is a 1-dimensional linear subspace of TxM 3}.
The disjoint union G1(M 3) := S
x∈M 3 G1
x forms a smooth fibre bundle over M 3, which is called the
1-dimensional Grassmannian bundle of M. Denote by (x, E) the point in G1(M 3) and by eπ the natural
projection from G1(M 3) to M 3, that is, eπ(x, E) = x. In addition, the flow φt and its tangent flow dφt
induce a continuous flow eφt on G1(M 3) as
eφt(x, E) = (φt(x), dφt(E)).
79

To complete the proof, we require the following claim, which was established in [97, Lemma 5.4].
Claim. Increasing P0 < PTOP(X|K, ϑ) if necessary, there exists r > 0 such that if ν is an X-invariant
measure on K satisfying Pν(X|K, ϑ) > P0, then ν(M(Sing(X), r)) < τ/2.
Proof. The proof is by contraction. Suppose that there are sequences of νn ∈P(X|K) and rn > 0 such
that rn →0, Pνn(X|K, ϑ) →PTOP(X|K, ϑ) and νn(M(Sing(X), rn)) ≥τ/2. Without loss of generality,
we may assume that νn →µ under the weak-∗topology. Therefore, µ is regular by Lemma 15.2. However,
νn(M(Sing(X), rn)) ≥τ/2 implies that µ(Sing(X)) ≥τ/2. This contraction proves the claim.
□
Let P0 and δ be given by the above claim. Then for any ε > 0, Λ := PESχ(ℓ, ε) \ Int(M(Sing(X), δ))
is a compact subset, where Int(M(Sing(X), δ)) is the interior of M(Sing(X), δ).
Moreover, for any
ν ∈Pe(X|K) with Pν(X|K, ϑ) > P0, we have ν(Λ) > τ/2. This completes the proof.
□
Based on Theorem 15.1 and 12.3, we immediately obtain the following corollary.
Corollary 15.6. Let M 3 be a closed 3-dimensional C∞Riemannian manifold and X be a C∞vector
field on M 3 having positive topological entropy.
• If B is a Borel homoclinic class of X and ϑ is a H¨older continuous potential on M 3 satisfying
Var(ϑ) < hTOP(X|B) = htop(X|B), then X is SPR for ϑ on B. As a corollary, there exists a
unique equilibrium state of ϑ for X on B.
• If ϑ is a H¨older continuous potential on M 3 satisfying Var(ϑ) < htop(X), then X is SPR for ϑ on
M 3. As a corollary, there exist only finitely many ergodic equilibrium states of ϑ for X.
□

Theorem 15.11. (Theorem E) If ϑ is a H¨older continuous potential on M 3, Then there exists a C∞
open and dense subset U of X ∞

[73] (see also [65, 70]) that there exists a hyperbolic horseshoe Λ0 for X whose topological entropy can
be made arbitrarily close to htop(X). Then by using the technique in [54], we may also require that the
topological pressure of ϑ on Λ0 is arbitrarily close to Ptop(X, ϑ). Since the topological pressure of ϑ varies
lower semi-continuously on a hyperbolic horseshoe, we obtain that the topological pressure of ϑ on M 3
varies lower semi-continuously with respect to the vector field. Thus there is a C∞neighborhood U2 of
X such that Ptop(Y, ϑ) > P0 for all Y ∈U2.

ergodic measure ν satisfying Pν(Y, ϑ) ≥P0, the unique Borel homoclinic class carrying ν has good
asymptotics. In particular, every ergodic equilibrium state of ϑ for Y ∈UZX,n has good asymptotics.

16
Star flows
In this section, we assume M is a smooth closed Riemannian manifold of dimension at least 3, and
X is a star vector field on M.
16.1
Basic properties of star flows
Recall that a C1 vector field X has the star property if there exists a C1 neighborhood U of X such
that for all Y ∈U, all periodic orbits and all singularities of Y are hyperbolic. For star vector fields, we
have the following fundamental result [75]:
Theorem 16.1. [75] For any star vector field X, there is a C1 neighborhood U of X and numbers η, t0 > 0
such that for any periodic orbit γ of Y ∈U with period T ≥t0, if Nγ = ¯

χ0 > 0 such that for every vector field Y ∈U, any Y -invariant measure must be χ0-hyperbolic. It implies
that µ(PESY

denote it by u(µ).
Denote by X ∗(M) the set of C1 star vector fields. The following property holds for multi-singular
hyperbolic vector fields, which constitute an open dense set of X ∗(M). See [111, 18, 45].
Lemma 16.2. [45, Proposition 4.2] There is a C1 open and dense subset of U∗of X ∗(M) satisfying: for
every chain recurrent class C there is i such that for every regular invariant measure µ supported on C,
u(x) = i for µ-a.e. x.
For pressures of star vector fields, we have the following lemma.
Lemma 16.3. Let ϑ : M →R be a continuous function. Assume that Yn and X are star vector fields
such that Yn →X in the C1 topology, and νn ∈P(Yn), µ ∈P(X) satisfy νn →µ in the weak-∗topology.
Then
(1) lim supn→∞Pνn(Yn, ϑ) ≤Pµ(X, ϑ).
(2) lim supn→∞Ptop(Yn, ϑ) ≥Ptop(X, ϑ).
Proof. It is proved in [72] that the metric entropy of star vector fields is upper-semicontinuous. Then
Item (1) is an immediate corollary. The lower-semicontinuity of the topological entropy for star vector
fields is proved in [71, Theorem B]. By using the technique in [54] (see the proof of Theorem 15.11), one
may show that the topological pressure for star vector fields is also lower-semicontinuous.
□
16.2
Star flows with pressure gap
The main theorem of this section is the following:
Theorem 16.4. (Theorem F) For any H¨older continuous potential ϑ, there exists a C2 open and C1
dense subset U of X ∗
ϑ(M) such that for every X ∈U, every ergodic equilibrium state of ϑ is rapid mixing.
The proof of Theorem 16.4 follows a similar method to that of Theorem 15.11. The main difficulty
lies in handling the higher-dimensional case. However, thanks to the star condition, the linear Poincar´e
flow admits a uniform dominated splitting, which allows us to avoid lifting measures to the Grassmannian
bundle.
Define
L i := {µ : ∃νn ∈Pe(Yn) s.t. uY (νn) = i, Yn →X, νn →µ and Pνn(Yn, ϑ) →Ptop(X, ϑ)}.
For any µ ∈L i, according to the upper-semicontinuity of metric pressure of star vector fields
(Lemma 16.3 (1)), µ is an equilibrium state of ϑ for X. Then similar to Lemma 15.2, by using the
pressure gap condition supx∈Sing(X) ϑ(x) < Ptop(X, ϑ), we have µ is regular.
85

theorem by applying Lemma 16.5 and adapting the proof of [36, Proposition 2.21, Theorem 3.1] with
